## E.7. Monitoring, reporting and evaluation arrangements (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

The Monitoring and Evaluation Plan is provided as Annex 11. The Plan includes GCF-level Impact and Outcome indicators and Programme-level Outcome and Activity indicators, metrics for each indicator, methodology  for  data  collection,  baseline  information,  frequency  of  data  collection,  and  indicative resources needed to complete the plan. The Programme monitoring and evaluation will be conducted in accordance with CEPF's established procedures. Key Programme partners, especially the RIT and the CSO grantees will be responsible for providing timely and comprehensive reporting including technical results  and  financial  data  as  necessary  and  appropriate.  These  responsibilities  will  form  part  of  the contractual  agreements  between  CEPF  and  the  Programme  partners  and  detailed  monitoring requirements will be established based on their roles. All monitoring data being collected at the ground level by these partners will be aggregated for Programme-level reporting by the CEPF Secretariat.

The Monitoring and Evaluation Plan describes the following elements of project evaluation:

- A Programme inception workshop ,  involving AFD and CEPF will be held at the Programme start to ensure the Programme team understand and take ownership of the Programme's objectives and results.
- The CEPF Donor Council acts as the Programme Steering Committee for all CEPF projects. Meetings are  held  semi-annually  to  review  and  approve  Programme  annual  budget  and  work  plans,  discuss

<!-- image -->

F

<!-- image -->

implementation issues and identify solutions, and increase coordination and communication between key Programme partners.

- AFD, including staff from AFD's three offices in the region (in Comoros, Madagascar and Mauritius),will conduct annual supervision visits to selected Programme countries and to grantee field sites to assess Programme progress at first hand.
- The  CEPF Secretariat  will organize  semi-annual  supervision  missions to  the  hotspot  to  oversee development of grant portfolios and review the performance of the Regional Implementation Team (RIT).
- The RIT will  continuously  monitor  and  evaluate  the  work  of  grantees.  They  will  ensure  quality  of performance by grantees and verify completion of deliverables as set out in grantees' proposals. To achieve  this  the  RIT  will  organize  regular  site  visits  to  grantees  to  monitor  their  progress,  ensure outreach, verify compliance and support capacity building. They will also provide guidance to grantees for the effective design and implementation of safeguard policies in compliance with guidelines detailed in the CEPF Operations Manual, which are consistent with GCF's safeguard policies.
- The NDAs (or designated relevant technical agencies - country specific arrangements to put in place) in each of the Programme countries will participate in monitoring and evaluation of Programme activities through participation at key meetings with AFD, the CEPF Secretariat and RIT, and supervision visits to grantee subproject field sites to assess Programme progress.
- CEPF will prepare an Annual Performance Report (APR) to monitor progress made and report to AFD. The APR will summarize the annual Programme results and progress.
- CEPF will draft a final report at the end of the Programme.
- The Programme will undergo an independent Interim  Evaluation after  5  years.  The  Evaluation  will determine progress being made toward the achievement of outcomes and outputs and will identify course correction if needed. It will include a socio-economic survey, to evaluate the Programme's impacts on human well-being, in particular the relevant results at Fund and Progamme levels. Recommendations of the Interim Evaluation will be incorporated into the design of the Programme to secure maximum results and sustainability during subsequent Programme implementation.
- An independent Final Evaluation will  take  place at the end of the project, and will be undertaken in accordance with AFD guidance. The Final Evaluation will focus on the delivery of the Programme's results as initially planned (and as corrected after the Interim Evaluation, if any such correction took place).  Once again, it will include a socio-economic survey to provide independent evaluation of human well-being impacts.
- Lessons learned and knowledge generation from the Programme will be disseminated within and beyond the Programme countries through information-sharing networks and forums developed by the Programme as described under Component 3.

In addition to the Programme specific monitoring of the indicators noted in Section E, CEPF will apply its global monitoring framework that measures impacts related to biodiversity, civil society, human well-being and creating the enabling conditions necessary for sustained ecosystem conservation. The indicators used in CEPF's monitoring framework will also allow easy reporting to the Governments of Programme countries on  SDG  and  Aichi  targets  (related  to  the  Convention  on  Biological  Diversity).  Further  details  on  the monitoring framework are provided in Annex 11.

## RISK ASSESSMENT AND MANAGEMENT

## F.1. Risk factors and mitigations measures (max. 3 pages)

Risk factors associated with the Programme include operational, financial, political and sustainability risks. Prominent risks that may affect the Programme are:

- -Lack of interest among CSOs to undertake EbA activities ;
- -Poor financial management impedes ability of CSOs to implement EbA activities;
- -Political instability impedes implementation in Programme countries;

<!-- image -->

F

<!-- image -->

- -Political space for civil society to influence public policy constricted in Programme countries;
- -Lack of suitable organizations to become long-term implementation structures;
- -Resources for long-term EbA financing sources not available.

## 1. Lack of interest among CSOs to undertake EbA activities

| Category                  | Probability   | Impact   |
|---------------------------|---------------|----------|
| Technical and operational | Low           | Medium   |

## Description

Operational risks are low for this Programme. The main risks come from the availability, capacity and interest of CSOs to engage in the EbA activities that are identified as priorities. Since CEPF has already worked in the hotspot targeted by the Programme (by up to 20 years in the case of Madagascar), the capacity and availability of many CSOs working in the environment sector is already known (see Annex 2, which includes descriptions of the state of CSO capacity in each country). Nevertheless, EbA is a relatively new field, and EbA activities have yet to be implemented at scale in any of the target countries. Therefore, the level of interest  among  CSOs  to  undertake  EbA  activities  and,  thus,  the  uptake  of  opportunities  under  the Programme have yet to be tested. However, CEPF has issued seven calls for proposals over the last five years in  the  Madagascar and Indian Ocean Islands Hotspot. Under these calls, CSOs in all four target countries have expressed an interest in undertaking EbA activities. In a few cases, where the objectives of these activities aligned closely with the biodiversity conservation goals of the current programme, they were supported. There is clearly unmet demand for funding for EbA activities. Based on the interest expressed to date in EbA, AFD and CEPF judge that there is a 'low' probability of the risk factor.

## Mitigation Measure(s)

CSOs  active  in  the  environment  sector  in  the  target  countries  were  consulted  during  the  Programme preparation phase, and confirmed that the planned activities align with their interests and capabilities. More extensive consultations will take place during the preparation of the Ecosystem Profile, which will define the priority areas for the award of EbA grants under Component 2 of the Programme. One of the criteria for selecting  priority  areas  will  be  availability  of  suitably  experienced  CSOs  interested  to  implement  EbA activities.

## 2. Poor financial management impedes ability of CSOs to implement EbA activities

| Category                  | Probability   | Impact   |
|---------------------------|---------------|----------|
| Technical and operational | Low           | Medium   |

Description

CSOs eligible for funding from CEPF range from small community-based groups to international NGOs and differ enormously in their experience and capacity in managing grant funding. Nevertheless, financial risks are 'low' for the Programme due to the operating procedures CEPF has established and refined over the last 20 years of grant making to CSOs.

## Mitigation Measure(s)

CEPF has well established grant management and oversight procedures that mitigate financial risk. The organization selected to perform the role of the RIT must demonstrate significant experience in financial management and oversight of a small grant program. The RIT must follow all CEPF policies, terms and conditions and ensure that sub-grantees comply with these also. The CEPF Secretariat will provide training to the RIT to ensure that it is fully conversant with all CEPF operating procedures, as set out in the operational manual. The procedures to mitigate risk begin with thorough due diligence during the application process, to  ensure that grantees have the necessary financial management policies, processes and personnel in

<!-- image -->

F

place skills before grants are awarded. A financial risk assessment is undertaken of all grantees, with higher risk grantees being subjected to more stringent oversight, such as review of detailed transaction reports and payment  vouchers,  project  audits  and  financial  site  visits.  CEPF  and  the  RIT  will  also  provide  close monitoring  of  grantees,  to  ensure  compliance  with  financial  policies,  and  provide  training  and  hands-on support to strengthen their financial management capacity. Detailed information on how CEPF manages financial  risks  in  its  grant  making  is  provided  in  the  Operations  Manual,  Annex  21.  CEPF's  systems  for managing risks, including financial risks, is an important part of the annual audits and periodic institutional assessments of CEPF that are conducted by the World Bank and serve all members of the CEPF donor council, including AFD. The World Bank evaluations have highlighted how successful CEPF has been at managing financial risks despite managing an extremely complex global portfolio that gets funding to small NGOs and community-based groups in countries that can have difficult working environments 44 .

| 3. Political instability impedes implementation in Programme countries   | 3. Political instability impedes implementation in Programme countries   | 3. Political instability impedes implementation in Programme countries   |
|--------------------------------------------------------------------------|--------------------------------------------------------------------------|--------------------------------------------------------------------------|
| Category                                                                 | Probability                                                              | Impact                                                                   |
| Other                                                                    | Medium                                                                   | Medium                                                                   |
| Description                                                              | Description                                                              | Description                                                              |

Political risks are medium for the Programme. Some degree of political change in the target countries is inevitable over the duration of a 10-year Programme. It seems unlikely that there will be substantial policy changes that remove political support for EbA measures, particularly given the likelihood of more visible climate change impacts, which ought to raise adaptation up the list of government priorities. Nevertheless, the hotspot contains countries with a recent history of political instability, and political crises or coups could prevent work in a country or, at minimum, impede civil society's engagement with government partners.

## Mitigation Measure(s)

CEPF has wide experience of supporting civil society in countries undergoing or emerging from political instability, and will continue to engage in such countries, provided opportunities to deliver the Programme's outcomes exist and the security situation does not present unacceptable risks to staff or partners. In practice, the focus on CSOs for the delivery of EbA activities means that implementation is less likely to be impacted by political events than is the case with projects or programs that depend on significant government agency involvement.  The  regional  focus  of  the  Programme  provides  another  mitigation  measure:  if  continued engagement  in  a  target  country  became  untenable,  the  Programme  would  focus  activities  in  the  other countries of the hotspot during the period of political instability.

| 4. Political space for civil society to influence public policy constricted in Programme countries   | 4. Political space for civil society to influence public policy constricted in Programme countries   | 4. Political space for civil society to influence public policy constricted in Programme countries   |
|------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------|------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------|------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------|
| Category                                                                                             | Probability                                                                                          | Impact                                                                                               |
| Technical and Operational                                                                            | Medium                                                                                               | Medium                                                                                               |

Description

The political space available for civil society is expanding in most countries in the hotspot, enabling them to have greater influence on public policy. However, relationships between government and civil society are dynamic,  and  political  space  for  civil  societies  can  be  constricted  if  they  are  perceived  as  moving  into sensitive areas. Therefore, this is considered a medium risk for the Programme.

## Mitigation Measure(s)

During  the  Programme  preparation  phase,  the  Nationally  Designated  Authorities  and  other  government stakeholders in the target countries were consulted and confirmed that the Programme activities align with their interests and national priorities. Further and more extensive consultations with these stakeholders will take place during the development of the Ecosystem Profile and Long-term Vision under Component 1 of the Programme, to ensure that EbA grant making priorities align with priorities identified in NAPAs, NAPs, NDCs  and  other  national  climate  change  related  strategies.  Furthermore,  the  Programme  will  deliver

<!-- image -->

F

targeted capacity building to CSOs to develop the necessary capacity and credibility to engage constructively with government partners. This risk will be further mitigated through careful selection of civil society partners with a track record of constructive partnership with government, and by fully involving government partners in the framing of policy questions addressed by the Programme, as well as selection of demonstration sites, and the integration of the ensuing lessons into the policy process.

| 5. Lack of suitable organizations to become long-term implementation structures   | 5. Lack of suitable organizations to become long-term implementation structures   | 5. Lack of suitable organizations to become long-term implementation structures   |
|-----------------------------------------------------------------------------------|-----------------------------------------------------------------------------------|-----------------------------------------------------------------------------------|
| Category                                                                          | Probability                                                                       | Impact                                                                            |
| Other- Sustainability                                                             | Low                                                                               | Medium                                                                            |

Description

CEPF has been working with the current RIT in the Madagascar and Indian Ocean Islands Hotspot since 2015. However, the host organization does not necessarily have an institutional agenda sufficiently aligned with the objectives of the Programme or the capacity mix necessary to become a long-term steward of the long-term vision for EbA in the hotspot. Therefore, it will be necessary to find other organizations that are better  placed  to  take  on  the  role  of  long-term  implementation  structure.  Given  the  large  number  of organizations working in the environment and natural resources sectors in the target countries, it should be possible to find a single organization or consortium of suitable organizations interested to take on this role. The target countries, and Madagascar in particular, are among the highest priorities in the world for the conservation  of  biodiversity.  Consequently,  they  have  attracted  significant  investment  in  biodiversity conservation for decades, enabling several international CSOs, as well as a growing number of national organizations, to establish capable programs. In recent years, the mandates of some of these organizations have broadened, in response to the climate crisis, and they have incorporated climate change mitigation and adaptation actions in their programs. It is from this pool of organizations, that one or more will be identified to become the long-term implementation structure. Hence, the probability of this risk is assessed as low.

## Mitigation Measure(s)

To mitigate this risk, a detailed stakeholder mapping will be conducted as part of the work to choose a longterm implementation structure. Suitably qualified organizations that express an interest in being part of the long-term implementation structure will be involved in the long-term vision exercise, so that they can take ownership  of  this  vision  and  ensure  that  it  is  aligned  with  their  missions.  A  model  for  long-term implementation  structures  has  been  developed  by  CEPF  that  accommodates  differences  in  institutional landscapes among countries. Given the differences in development of the civil society sector among the four target countries, it may be necessary to build a consortium that brings together complementary skills from organizations across the hotspot.

| 6. Resources for long-term EbA finance sources not available   | 6. Resources for long-term EbA finance sources not available   | 6. Resources for long-term EbA finance sources not available   |
|----------------------------------------------------------------|----------------------------------------------------------------|----------------------------------------------------------------|
| Category                                                       | Probability                                                    | Impact                                                         |
| Other- Sustainability                                          | Medium                                                         | Medium                                                         |

## Description

A key pillar of sustainability of the Programme will be to develop resource mobilization strategies to generate additional revenues for EbA activities led by civil society, including from non-traditional sources, such as the private sector. The availability of funding for climate change adaptation from institutional donors and other traditional  sources  of  environmental  financing  is  increasing  in  the  target  countries.  To  date,  however, relatively  little  of  this  funding  has  been  made  available  for  EbA.  Instead,  most  climate  change  financial resources have been directed towards mitigation activity and the energy sector in particular. Nevertheless, there appears to be a growing recognition of the importance of nature-based solutions to climate change, which should mean more financial resources to support EbA are available in the future.  the risk of resources for long-term EbA financing not being available, applies to funds needed after the implementation period, to sustain and amplify EbA approaches through long-term financing mechanisms. It is anticipated that these mechanisms will be established towards the end of the implementation period (10 years) and continue at least until the end of the programme lifespan (20 years). These long-term financing mechanisms may not necessarily follow the model of an endowment fund, when significant funding must be mobilized up front.

<!-- image -->

F

<!-- image -->

Rather,  they  may  follow  payment  for  ecosystem  services  (PES),  voluntary  contributions  for  private companies or other similar models, where funding is mobilized at a lower level on a recurring basis. For this reason, the amount of funding that needs to be mobilized during the programme lifespan, to launch these mechanisms, may be relatively modest.

## Mitigation Measure(s)

This risk will be mitigated through an analysis of the availability of traditional and non-traditional sources of EbA financing in the second five-year phase of the Programme, so that the available and potential resources are known. For example, there may be opportunities for partnership with the private sector that have not been adequately considered. One potential area to explore as part of Component 3 is whether there are policy incentives that could be used (or disincentives that can be removed) so that the enabling environment changes to encourage partnerships between CSOs and the private sector for long-term EbA implementation. The  risk  is  further  mitigated  through  the  design  of  the  Programme,  which  integrates  strategies  for sustainability throughout. For example, the development of a Long-term Vision and the associated financial plan will be done through participatory processes involving government, private sector and major donors operating in the Programme countries. This should ensure that there is a broad consensus on needs and institutional buy-in to provide long-term financial support to the effective EbA approaches developed under the Programme.

## Programme Grievance Mechanism

CEPF requires all subprojects to have in place a grievance mechanism that must make available the contact details of the RIT and CEPF Secretariat, in case people affected by the project have concerns that they do not wish to raise directly with the grantee. Any grievances raised with the grantee should be communicated to the RIT and the CEPF Grant Director within 15 days, together with a proposed response. If the claimant is still not satisfied following the response, the grievance may be submitted directly to the CEPF Executive Director via the dedicated email account (cepfexecutive@conservation.org) or by mail. If the claimant is still not satisfied, the grievance may be submitted to AFD at their local office .

<!-- image -->

## GCF POLICIES AND STANDARDS

## G.1. Environmental and social risk assessment (max. 750 words, approximately 1.5 pages)

AFD, a long-term donor to CEPF, assesses CEPF's activities as category C risk (see certification provided as Appendix A of Annex 6). Annex 6 includes an environmental and social risk screening of the Programme to  check  risk  factors  aligned  to  GCF's  environmental  and  social  safeguards.  Based  on  this  screening, subprojects proposed by CSOs are expected to be Category C. Although some Programme activities will occur  in  ecologically  sensitive  areas,  the  eligible  activities  will  be  ones  to  protect  or  restore  natural ecosystems and therefore subprojects likely to have adverse impacts will not be considered. Similarly, the Programme will not support activities likely to result in adverse social impacts due to involuntary restriction of  access  to  land  or  natural  resources.  Involuntary  restrictions  should  be  minimized  because  CSO subprojects are expected to have strong buy-in from stakeholders in the communities where they are located. Nevertheless, CEPF has a Process Framework for Involuntary Restrictions that  applies  to  all  grants provided by CEPF, including those under this Programme, and it is included in Annex 6.

The Programme will work through financial intermediation for the majority of its activities, including all onthe- ground activities. CEPF will be responsible for providing grants to Civil Society Organizations (CSOs) who  will  be  responsible  for  the  EbA  activities.  Therefore,  following  GCF's  categorization  process  the Programme is considered to have a low-level risk through intermediation, or I3 .

CEPF applies its Environmental and Social Management Framework (ESMF) to prevent, minimize, and mitigate potential adverse environmental and social effects of individual subprojects (provided as part of Annex 6). CEPF will appraise subprojects proposed by grantees not only on their technical merit, but also on their environmental and social ramifications. Therefore, procedures for addressing environmental and social issues are included in the subproject cycle management process. A driving principle of CEPF is to prevent and mitigate any harm to people and thus to incorporate environmental and social concerns as an intrinsic part of subproject cycle management.

The ESMF explains the CEPF environmental and social assessment processes. It also includes a Pest Management Plan covering use of pesticides, a process framework to further elaborate safeguards specific to Indigenous  Peoples (although  this  is  unlikely  to  apply  in  the  hotspot  where  there  very  few  groups considered as Indigenous Peoples), and a process framework to determine safeguard measures when a grantee subproject may result in restriction of access to natural resources.

Environmental and social safeguards will be tracked during all stages of the subproject cycle with the main objective  of  ensuring  that  supported  activities  comply  with  the  policies  and  guidelines  laid  out  in  the Operational  Manual  and  with  the  GCF's  environmental  and  social  safeguard  policies.  This  includes confirming that measures are incorporated into subproject design to prevent, minimize, and mitigate potential adverse environmental and social effects of individual subprojects.

CEPF has also adopted a best practice on stakeholder engagement for grantees, which will also be used for this Programme (provided as part of Annex 21). Stakeholder engagement is a fundamental principle of good subproject design, and best practice consists of involving all stakeholders, including local communities and other project-affected people, as well as government, private sector and civil society partners, as early as possible in the preparation process and ensuring that their views and concerns are made known and taken into account. The CEPF Secretariat will ensure that all CEPF-funded subprojects comply with this best practice. In the case of large grants, this will mean working directly with applicants and grantees. In the case of small grants, this will mean providing training and oversight to the Regional Implementation Team (RIT), to  ensure  that  they  are  providing  appropriate  guidance  to  applicants  and  grantees.  A report  on  the stakeholder engagement undertaken in the preparation of the Programme has been provided as Annex 7 along with the stakeholder engagement plan for use during the Programme.

<!-- image -->

G

<!-- image -->

## G.2. Gender assessment and action plan (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

CEPF carries out its mission through a gender equity lens and has an established gender policy (see Annex 8).  This  means that staff of the CEPF Secretariat, the Regional Implementation Team and grantees will understand and take into account the different roles of men and women in CEPF-related activities at all scales  (e.g.,  Regional  Implementation  Team  training,  proposal  design,  project  implementation  and reporting). Gender issues and considerations are actively incorporated throughout the grant-making process and progress on gender-related outcomes are monitored.

A Gender Assessment and Action Plan is provided as Annex 8. Given that specific CSO subprojects have not been identified at this stage and will be the subject of further analysis and priority setting, it is not possible to identify specific vulnerabilities at this stage. The assessment therefore focused on identifying important gender related themes at the national level for each of the Programme countries, based on literature review. Further gender assessment and specific recommendations linked to identified EbA priorities will be done as part of the ecosystem profiling process in Component 1. Specific gender issues related to subprojects must be identified by applicant grantees at the subproject proposal stage.

Gender inequality remains a significant problem in some of the countries in the Programme, increasing the risks and burdens of climate change for women. By contrast, in Mauritius and Seychelles, per capita national incomes are higher than the other countries and women enjoy greater equality in many aspects of society. However, in Comoros and Madagascar, women are often unequal participants in decision-making, labor markets, legal processes and they face gender stereotypes due to socio-cultural norms. Women are also often poorer than men and therefore have less access to adaptation options when faced with risk events (e.g. poor crop yields, floods, droughts etc.). Not only are women often disadvantaged in terms of human development indicators, many are also highly dependent on natural resource for their livelihoods. Women are often responsible within their households for securing water, food and fuel for cooking. Their livelihoods are  therefore  those  that  are  often  first  and  most  effected  by  climate  change.  In  rural  areas  of  all  the Programme countries, the different roles and responsibilities of men and women in society, and different gender norms mean that they can be impacted by climate change in different ways.  For example, in the agricultural context, women tend to care for household gardens and subsistence agriculture, while men are more  likely  to  engage  in  cash  generating  activities.  Women  often  therefore  play  an  important  role  in sustainable resource management, which is particularly relevant for projects such as this one promoting improved environmental stewardship such as through EbA.

Ensuring that both men and women have equal opportunities to participate in, and benefit from, the GCF Programme  will  be  achieved  through  progressive  and  efficient  mainstreaming  of  gender  dimensions throughout the grant-making processes in the biodiversity hotspots. One of the goals of the Gender Action Plan is to ensure that, for relevant grants, any gender-related adverse impact is avoided, minimized and/or mitigated. Depending on the type of intervention and scope of activities, the degree of relevance of gender dimensions may vary. Similarly, depending on the capacities and interest of the grantees, the level of gender mainstreaming opportunity may vary. The objective of the Gender Action Plan (see Annex 8) is to outline actions that will be specifically taken within the duration of the Programme.

To ensure that all Programme activities (e.g., definition of investment strategies, training of the Regional Implementation Team, subproject design, implementation and reporting, monitoring and evaluation, learning and communication) are consistent with CEPF's Gender Policy, the Plan is divided into three parts:

- 1) Preparation of the organizational structure and necessary tools.
- 2)  Implementation throughout the GCF Programme.

<!-- image -->

<!-- image -->

G

<!-- image -->

- 3) Monitoring &amp; Evaluation and dissemination of lessons learned (especially relevant to Component 3, which concerns replicating successful approaches to other countries in the Programme and beyond to other hotspots where CEPF is active).

CEPF recognizes that grantees have different capacities, needs and experiences in integrating gender into their subprojects and operational structures. CEPF uses a gender tracking tool (see Annex 21 section OM 4.4.4) that can be used by an organization to understand if, and to what extent, gender considerations have been integrated into its program and operations. It can then be used by CEPF to determine interest in learning about gender issues. The Gender Action Plan seeks to be practical in terms of feasibility given the broad  geographical  targets  of  the  GCF  Programme,  the  wide  variation  in  capacities  of  the  civil  society organizations that are expected to be engaged and supported by the Programme, and the gender disparities among the countries involved in the Programme.

## G.3. Financial management and procurement (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

AFD, as the AE, will be responsible for the project implementation and activities carried out by the EE. AFD will  provide  oversight  of  procurement  function,  prior  review  and  approval,  and  financial  management  of CI/CEPF  as  detailed  in  the  AMA.  AFD  will  also  provide  oversight  of  the  financial  management  of  the Programme. Procurement activities and processes will follow AFD procurement policy and standards, as detailed in the AMA.

CI/CEPF's financial management and procurement policies and systems are described in detail in the CEPF Operations Manual provided as Annex 21.

Conservation International (CI) oversees internal control and financial management of CEPF in accordance with CI's financial policies and procedures. CI uses Unit4 Business World as  its  accounting  and  human resources software for both its headquarters and field offices. Unit4 Business World's financial management package is an industry leading integrated set of financial management and accounting applications. CI has established a coding structure within its general ledger to track CEPF funds. CI maintains CEPF funds in a segregated USD-denominated bank account.

A separate audit of CEPF records, accounts, and financial statements is undertaken annually, in accordance with generally accepted accounting principles. The purpose of this external audit is to provide assurance on the financial statements of CEPF. The audit will test CEPF's compliance with certain provisions of the CEPF Operational  Manual  and  consideration  of  its  related  internal  control.  This  external  CEPF  audit  will  be conducted by independent auditors in accordance with Terms of Reference approved by the CEPF Donor Council (Annex 21, section 2.1.1).

CEPF's procurement policy is provided in the CEPF Operational Manual (Annex 21, section 2.3). It is based on the principle that all purchases of goods and services must be made with complete impartiality based solely on the merits of supplier proposals, including criteria such as efficiency, quality, reliability, reputation, cost, delivery and payment terms.   Prior to undertaking any purchases of goods or services with CEPF funds,  grantees/recipients  are  required  to  have  institutional  procurement  policies  in  effect  that  are substantially the same as those of the CEPF procurement policy. CEPF also has a written ethics standards policy (Annex 21, section 2.4) that applies to all persons and entities that receive, are responsible for the deposit or transfer of, or take or influence decisions regarding the use of Grant Funds received from CEPF.

As a grant-making fund, CEPF applies various tools to assess and mitigate financial and procurement risk. These include assessment of the adequacy of prospective grantees' accounting policies and procedures and grants management practices and assessment to ensure various anti-money laundering and counter-terrorist financing legislation and related donor obligations. Prior to any grant award, CI must be assured that the proposed grantee has sound financial controls and reporting systems to ensure that all subproject funds are

<!-- image -->

G

<!-- image -->

expended prudently and accounted for appropriately.  CI must also be assured that the proposed grantee institution is able and willing to comply, and (as applicable) is able and willing to ensure compliance by any subgrantees,  with  CI  policies,  applicable  donor  terms  and  conditions,  and  local  laws  and  regulations.  CI  may condition  funding  on  the  implementation  of  certain  practices  or  improvements.  CEPF  also  applies  a  risk assessment that has been developed to assess whether a proposed recipient of a large grant is low, medium, or high risk so that appropriate monitoring and audit procedures can be applied.

Proposal name:

## I. Overall assessment of the Secretariat

1. The funding proposal is presented to the Board for consideration with the following remarks.

| Strengths                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                           | Points of caution                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                 |
|---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------|---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------|
| The proposed pathway between ecosystem- based adaptation (EbA) actions and increased human resilience to stresses and shocks from climate change and other sources contribute to the high impact potential, which will be achieved through the implementation of cost-effective EbA at scale in highly vulnerable small island developing States, resulting in increased resilience for up to 700,000 beneficiaries | The local partners (civil society organizations), which will be implementing the activities through grants administered by Critical Ecosystems Partnership Fund (CEPF), may have limited technical capacity and institutional capacity. It is key for this programme to provide effective technical support, monitoring and a knowledge management tools framework. The proposed flexibility in the design of activities adequately reflects this |
| The programme has a strong chance to reach its sustainable development potential (defined as potential for externalities in the form of expected improvements), particularly through its contribution to the resilience of hotspots of endangered biodiversity in partnership with CEPF, a key player which can help scale up successful approaches                                                                 | The sustainability of each activity relies on the partnerships with the private sector and revenue models that are to be generated. Replicating these is a key barrier to scaling up the impact, as is common to other small grants programmes. The small grants programme attempts to address the barrier by fostering partnerships in a systematic manner through the proposed Component 3                                                      |
| Paradigm shift potential: strong focus on knowledge generation to enable local action, as well as a strong emphasis on partnering with the private sector to leverage financing at scale in each country. The CEPF portfolio (USD 16 million/year) offers a good replication opportunity because mainstreaming EbA positively influences biodiversity conservation activities funded through the CEPF               | EbA activities often include ecosystem restoration activities, which have significantly higher costs than protecting existing natural ecosystems. There is, therefore, a tendency to focus on protecting remaining natural ecosystems rather than engaging in the challenges of ecosystem restoration                                                                                                                                             |

2. The Board may wish to consider approving this funding proposal with the terms and conditions listed in the respective term sheet and addendum XVIII, titled 'List of proposed conditions and recommendations'.

## II. Summary of the Secretariat's assessment

## 2.1 Project background and climate rationale

3. Small island developing States (SIDS) across the globe are highly vulnerable to climate change, which poses severe threats to livelihoods, infrastructure, and marine and terrestrial ecosystems. Ecosystem-based adaptation (EbA) approaches have proven to be highly successful to cost-effectively reduce the vulnerability of people and ecosystems to climate change by leveraging the value of ecosystem services (from water resource management to coastal protection), as well as by providing substantial environmental and social benefits such as the protection of biodiversity. This has led to EbA interventions being included in the national planning documents of many SIDS. There remain challenges to mainstreaming such approaches: for example, in the ability to leverage private financing to complement country budgets and grant financing, and the technical capacity and knowledge to design effective EbA interventions which are highly specific to a country's physical and institutional circumstances.
4. The proposed programme targets four island countries in the Indian Ocean - Comoros, Madagascar, Mauritius and Seychelles - with a combined population of 29 million people. These countries have identified EbA interventions as high priorities in their climate change strategies, and have a track record of international projects in the areas of ecosystem conservation and EbA, including projects funded by GCF (Blue Action Fund in Madagascar) and the Adaptation Fund. The four countries are part of the Madagascar and Indian Ocean biodiversity hotspot, with livelihoods highly dependent on the rich ecosystems and natural resources, whether dominated by agriculture and fishing (as in the case of Comoros and Madagascar) or tourism and the services sector (Mauritius and Seychelles).
5. The funding proposal identifies key areas of climate vulnerability in each of the countries. For example, reduced rainfall in the rainy season and increased temperatures are predicted to affect the availability of water resources for agriculture, human consumption and ecological flows, as well as overall agricultural yields; while the increased intensity of cyclones will impact critical water supply infrastructure, agricultural supply chains and coastal erosion. Coastal and marine ecosystems on which fisheries depend are also expected to suffer the impacts of rising water temperatures and cyclones, affecting in turn the livelihoods of the populations in the target countries. A summary of the impacts is provided in section 3 of the feasibility study.
6. The programme identifies several categories of EbA intervention that have proven effectiveness in mitigating against the adverse effects of climate change and increasing the resilience of populations and ecosystems. These include:
5. (a)
6. (b)
7. (c)

Protection and restoration of watershed forests that perform critical flood prevention, soil stabilization and catchment protection functions; Piloting and replication of climate-resilient agroforestry models, using native plant species for shade, ground cover and nutrient fixing; Preservation of traditional and indigenous knowledge and natural resource management practices;

(d) Diversification and strengthening of livelihood assets and strategies, including through sustainable fisheries management, nature-based tourism, value chains for natural products, and so on; and

<!-- image -->

- (e) Restoration of small island ecosystems through eradication and control of alien invasive species.
7. The programme will be executed by the Critical Ecosystems Partnership Fund (CEPF), a key player in the field of biodiversity conservation, established in 2000 within Conservation International Foundation (CI) as a mechanism to enable civil society organizations (CSOs) to support conservation of critical ecosystems within biodiversity hotspots. CEPF will leverage its strong network of partners and experience of working with CSOs and governments in the target countries, as well as its capabilities to generate and share knowledge and replicate successful interventions in other biodiversity hotspots. As a result of the project, CEPF operations will be able to incorporate the climate resilience angle using the knowledge generated, increasing the capacity of local actors to implement EbA interventions in biodiversity hotspots around the world.
8. The programme will operate within the standard CEPF framework. The CEPF secretariat will support and oversee grant-making in the hotspots in line with the investment plans developed for each key biodiversity area in Component 1. CEPF will also ensure compliance with GCF policies, and it will be responsible for monitoring and communicating results. The regional implementation team, based locally and selected through a competitive process, will provide support and local coordination, and will manage the process of awarding, for small grants (under USD 50,000). National designated authorities (NDAs) will be involved in the grant awarding process in each country.

## 2.2 Component by component analysis

## Component 1: Developing strategic plans for ecosystem-based adaptation in the small island biodiversity hotspots that are well aligned with national climate change strategies (total cost: USD 812,124; no GCF financing)

9. This component, which will be fully funded by a grant from CEPF and implemented directly by CEPF, will set up the main lines of intervention in each of the key biodiversity areas by updating the ecosystem profile, which provides a detailed strategy for investment in conservation and restoration of critical ecosystems over a short time frame, based on identifying critical ecosystem services and consultation with local stakeholders, and which will be updated through activity 1.1. Through the project, a long-term vision of CEPF investment will be developed, with the aim of an eventual transition to other sources of support, and an exit strategy in which civil society has the capacity and credibility to carry out the work on its own. Critically, this will be developed alongside a financing plan for each hotspot describing the funding requirements for the implementation, guiding fundraising efforts by CEPF and other donors, and including potential non-traditional sources of long-term financing for EbA activities, most importantly the private sector. The identification of opportunities for private sector

<!-- image -->

- partnerships with CSOs to deliver EbA, which will be the focus of activity 1.5, is critical to delivering this vision and integral to the paradigm shift of the programme.
- Component 2: Supporting ecosystem-based adaptation activities through grants to civil society organizations (total cost: USD 43,903,614; GCF cost: USD 34,553,993, or 78.7 per cent)
10. This component will deliver EbA interventions on the ground, in line with the strategies and eligibility criteria set out in the ecosystem profile for each key biodiversity area, through subgrants provided to CSOs.
11. The component will consist of two subcomponents. Subcomponent 2.1 is aimed at setting up and operating the mechanisms for awarding grants in each of the countries, including the setting up of the regional implementation team, which will support the mechanism in the country, the awarding of grants, and the supervision, monitoring and evaluation of the portfolio. Applications will mainly be generated through open calls for proposals, widely advertised through online and mainstream media, as well as local dissemination of the information; while imbalances in the portfolio will be addressed through targeted grants. The selection of proposals will involve peer review by external reviewers and the participation of the NDA. Of the total amount of the component, USD 30.4 million (69.2 per cent of the grant amount for the component) will be in the form of grants to CSOs for the implementation of EbA activities on the ground, and an additional USD 5 million (11.4 per cent) will be for monitoring and evaluation of activities. Eligibility criteria for participation will be fine-tuned in component 1, but will include, in addition to the priority areas of intervention identified in the ecosystem profile and the priorities in national climate change policy, the following: the contribution to achieving the GCF investment criteria and particularly addressing climate vulnerability based on a clear climate change risk; positive gender impacts; efficient use of funds; and a clear strategy for achieving financial sustainability.
12. The second subcomponent aims to address knowledge gaps in the science behind EbA approaches in each biodiversity hotspot and the effectiveness of promising EbA interventions, in association with academic institutions, through applied research activities. This subcomponent is expected to address a key barrier to EbA effectiveness globally by generating information to guide policy decisions about EbA in the programme countries. This component will also include activities to ensure rigorous, science-based quantification and verification of the impacts of the grant portfolio on ecosystem services.
- Component 3: Ensuring long-term sustainability and replicating success through knowledge products and tools for ecosystem-based adaptation (total cost: USD 2,141,405; GCF cost: USD 1,636,884, or 76.4 per cent)
13. This capacity-building component has two main aims. The first is to facilitate wider replication of successful models and tools demonstrated in a hotspot under the first two components, including to other hotpots where CEPF is active, by disseminating the knowledge through South-South exchanges, partnerships between CSOs and private sector actors, and audio-visual products, such as short videos, webinars and websites. Significant value can be gained by leveraging the central role of CEPF in the network of partners in biodiversity conservation to mainstream climate change adaptation principles in biodiversity conservation interventions, effectively linking both areas.
14. The second aim of the component, responding to the significant technical and institutional challenges of implementing EbA in the long term (such as coordination among stakeholders, conflict resolution, etc.), is to provide CSOs and institutions with the capacity to implement the long-term visions described in component 1. The project proposes to do so, in addition to more traditional capacity-building of the CSOs to design and implement EbA activities, through the creation of long-term, multi-stakeholder implementation structures that act as steward of the strategies; the development of resource mobilization strategies; and the development of models for private sector finance. If successful, the lines of intervention

<!-- image -->

GCF/B.26/02/Add.07

Page 75

proposed can address key barriers to scaling up EbA and help to unlock sustainable finance flows towards climate change adaptation and ecosystem conservation, which would be the key paradigm shift outcome of the project. Such partnerships have so far been limited; the project describes several examples with mining companies in Madagascar that could serve as models, but it does not describe in sufficient detail how such partnerships could materialize. It is expected that, as with the innovative approach they represent, such activities may have varying levels of success, and it is important that adequate adaptive management and lessons learned are implemented for the benefit of similar future interventions.

## III. Assessment of performance against investment criteria

## 3.1 Impact potential Scale: High

15. The programme's core objective is to protect and restore coastal, freshwater and terrestrial ecosystems that are providing critical ecosystem services for vulnerable people in the four programme countries. The funding proposal estimates that the programme, through the grants to CSOs, will be able to restore or improve the management of 1.89 million hectares of highly biodiverse ecosystems in the Indian Ocean hotspot, with 88,000 direct beneficiaries, through the employment opportunities generated and/or from support to adopt diversified, climate-resilient livelihood options. Including 610,000 indirect beneficiaries, a total of 698,000 beneficiaries will see an increase in their climate change resilience, access to improved quantity and quality of ecosystem services and income. The large impacts expected on both the livelihood and ecosystem dimensions, based on the prior experience of CEPF, justify a high rating for the programme's impact potential. In addition, research suggests that the protection of coastal and marine ecosystems such as mangroves, salt marshes, seagrass beds and seaweeds may have a significant effect in reducing emissions from land use, although this impact has not been quantified.

## 3.2 Paradigm shift Scale: Medium-High

16. The programme has been designed with the objective of catalysing action on EbA by CSOs around the world, and aims to address the key barriers to design, finance and sustain interventions by local communities. The key features of the project to achieve the goals are as follows:
2. (a) A strong focus on research, through subcomponent 2.2, to address the knowledge gaps in relation to ecosystem functions or services, how communities benefit from those, and the effectiveness of EbA interventions in improving the provision of ecosystem services. To that end, the project will fund scientific studies partnering with local and
3. international academic institutions amounting to USD 3.5 million in grants to CSOs; (b) An embedded exit strategy at different levels to ensure the sustainability of each key biodiversity area. At the strategic level, the ecosystem profile will be updated with a long-term vision that plans investment by CEPF and the transition to fundraising capital and leveraging private sector finance. Long-term 'steward' institutions, composed of diverse stakeholders from government, civil society and the private sector, will be in charge of seeing that vision through, and capacity will be built to enable stakeholders to perform their roles in the design and financing of further interventions. At the subproject level, grantees will have to demonstrate a clear strategy for financial sustainability; and

(c) A plan to disseminate knowledge and lessons learned to CSOs, and directly build the technical capacity of at least 25 CSOs, which is well suited to the preeminent status of

<!-- image -->

- CEPF in the network of organizations working on ecosystem and biodiversity conservation.
17. The GCF contribution can deliver a paradigm shift on two fronts: first, by demonstrating how to create a sustainable enabling environment for civil society to act on ecosystem protection and climate change adaptation, with financial support from governments and the private sector; and second, by leveraging the experience and network of CEPF to disseminate results, while adding a climate adaptation dimension to their ample work on ecosystem protection. This paradigm shift should achieve replication at a larger scale, across a portfolio of approximately USD 16 million per year (over four times the GCF investment in a 10-year period), and result in an area under protection or sustainable management of 22.8 million hectares.
18. The medium-high rating is based on the risk that there is some uncertainty in the capacity of the programme to fully deliver partnerships with the private sector and securing the financial sustainability of the interventions. If successful, the paradigm shift should be considered as high. However, CEPF has a strong track record regarding the uptake of biodiversity conservation activities by local communities, with existing grantees supporting 14.8 million hectares of new protected areas, and improved management of 8 million hectares of production landscape.

## 3.3 Sustainable development potential Scale: High

19. The programme will promote the conservation and restoration of 1.89 million hectares in some of the most biodiverse island ecosystems on the planet in the four countries which jointly constitute the Indian Ocean biodiversity hotspot. It is expected that the programme will deliver major benefits in terms of the preservation of ecosystems and the biodiversity that dwells in them. The interventions proposed will also help to preserve water flows and water quality from climate change impacts, prevent soil erosion, or avoid deforestation, and will support the four countries to fulfil their obligations under the United Nations Convention on Biological Diversity and the Sendai Framework for Disaster Risk Reduction, and achieve their adaptation and mitigation targets under the United Nations Framework Convention on Climate Change.
20. The preservation of ecosystem services has wide benefits over the multiple dimensions of sustainable development and, as such, it is expected to help countries move towards the fulfilment of a number of sustainable development goals. The project will directly provide livelihoods and maintain access to ecosystem services for highly vulnerable rural populations (especially in Comoros and Madagascar) that often depend on ecosystems, and will promote sustainable economic growth by enabling the growth of business sectors that rely on healthy ecosystems (e.g. fisheries, agriculture, or tourism). It is expected that 88,000 people will benefit from jobs or improved livelihoods as a result of the programme. Although not quantified, due to the fact that specific interventions are yet to be defined, there is a high confidence that these benefits should be very relevant.

## 3.4 Needs of the recipient Scale: Medium-High

21. The programme targets four SIDS with high vulnerability to climate change, in particular Comoros and Madagascar, two least developed countries that rank among the most at risk according to the Notre Dame Global Adaptation Initiative (ND-GAIN) index comprising readiness and vulnerability. All countries have already reported significant impacts of climate change, including increased temperatures, rising sea levels, ocean acidification, coastal erosion,

<!-- image -->

- flooding and droughts, and climate change projections indicate a worsening trend over this century.
22. Lack of finance is a key barrier to adaptation and biodiversity conservation actions, which are dependent on donor finance and are also subject to strong manmade pressures. GCF financing will contribute to cover the financing gap while contributing to addressing capacity needs of civil society in the countries, as well as furthering their sustainable development and gender agendas.

## 3.5 Country ownership Scale: High

23. The programme is fully in line with the relevant climate adaptation and biodiversity conservation legislation, and it will help countries meet their obligations under the United Nations Convention for Biological Diversity.
24. The programme is designed to ensure that there is a strong level of involvement from all parts of civil society in the implementation of the actions, and indeed to empower civil society to be able to design, implement and finance EbA actions by the end of the programme. CSOs will be beneficiaries of subgrants and will be directly in charge of the design and execution of EbA interventions, while benefitting from capacity-building through training courses, workshops and South-South exchanges. NDAs will be involved in the process of awarding grants, building their capacity to manage a portfolio of interventions in the countries in the future. Finally, all sectors of society, including the private sector, will come together in the long-term implementation structures that will be responsible for safeguarding the plans for each key biodiversity area and ensuring their financial sustainability.
25. The executing entity, CEPF, has a well-established presence in all the countries in the Indian Ocean biodiversity hotspot, having provided 102 grants for a total of USD 10.3 million to CSOs for biodiversity conservation activities since 2015. As such, it is already a donor that is well known to many of the CSOs operating in the programme countries.

## 3.6 Efficiency and effectiveness Scale: Medium-High

26. Existing studies agree that EbA interventions are highly cost-effective solutions in increasing the resilience of populations, while delivering significant social and environmental co-benefits. The programme expects to deliver high value for money, improving the resilience of 698,000 people while preserving and restoring 1.89 million hectares of highly biodiverse ecosystems, through the GCF grant of USD 38 million.
27. The use of grants in this case is adequate because interventions will generally not be able to generate substantial reflows and are aimed at increasing the resilience of the most vulnerable.
28. Subgrants to CSOs, which form the core of the programme and amount to USD 30.4 million, will be financed by GCF and CEPF in a ratio of 2.6:1, with GCF also financing the operation of the grant-making mechanism and the evaluation of EbA impact and effectiveness. Co-financing from CEPF will be mostly aimed at developing the ecosystem profiles in component 1, and for the development of knowledge tools to replicate successful interventions and improve the capacity of civil society to deliver EbA interventions at scale. An unspecified amount of finance from the private sector will also flow in as a result of partnerships with the private sector in each hotspot and specific subprojects (where it will be part of the investment criteria but not mandatory), although this is not quantified and will be tracked as part of the programme's results.

<!-- image -->

GCF/B.26/02/Add.07 Page 78

29. Although the direct overall co-financing ratio (USD 3.4 from GCF per USD 1 in cofinancing) may fall slightly short of GCF ambition considering the participation of a wellestablished fund in the field of biodiversity, the programme will unlock a much larger volume of finance globally for adaptation as CEPF replicates EbA interventions following the principles developed and lessons learned in this project, adding a climate dimension to its portfolio that already amounts to USD 16 million a year in biodiversity hotspots around the world.

## IV. Assessment of consistency with GCF safeguards and policies

## 4.1 Environmental and social safeguards

30. The accredited entity (AE) has conducted an environmental and social risk screening of the programme in line with the GCF environmental and social safeguards, and considers the programme to have a low-level risk of intermediation 3 (I-3). The Secretariat confirms the environmental and social risk categorization and that the programme is within the AE's accreditation level of the AE. On the basis of this assessment, subprojects proposed by civil society organizations (CSOs) are expected to be under category C classification.
31. Although some of the programme's activities will occur in ecologically sensitive areas, the eligible activities are expected to conserve or enhance these ecosystems; and those activities that have interventions which will potentially have adverse impacts will not be eligible. In addition, involuntary restrictions of access to land or natural resources are not expected because participation and support from stakeholders in the communities will have to be acquired. The programme has likewise established an environmental and social risk screening process and an environmental and social management framework to ensure that proposals are continuously screened and that category A or B subprojects are excluded.
32. An indigenous peoples planning framework is provided as an annex to the environmental and social management framework: it outlines the screening and consultation processes that need to be undertaken, and the key responsibilities for both the CEPF secretariat and the subproject applicants. The project excludes activities that require land acquisition or relocation of local communities, or that affect indigenous peoples without having obtained their free, prior and informed consent.
33. The CEPF secretariat (hosted by Conservation International) and the RIT will implement the programme. The programme will work through financial intermediation for the majority of its activities, so any capacity-building and technical support to resolve issues that may be identified will be provided to those CSOs that have less experience of dealing with environmental and social safeguards.
34. The CEPF secretariat has also adopted a stakeholder engagement strategy for grantees of the programme, which will ensure that all the funded subprojects comply with best practice. Grievance redress mechanisms at the AE level as well as at the CEPF level are available to be accessed by local communities and other interested stakeholders that may have concerns about the programme. Dealing with grievances at the subproject level will also include local conflict resolution and grievance redress mechanisms for affected communities, and these should be developed in culturally appropriate ways and ensure adequate representation from vulnerable or marginalized sectors. In line with the GCF Indigenous Peoples Policy, the GCF Independent Redress Mechanism and the Secretariat's indigenous peoples focal point are also available for affected local communities.

## 4.2 Gender policy

<!-- image -->

GCF/B.26/02/Add.07

Page 79

35. The AE has provided a gender assessment and gender action plan, and therefore complies with the requirements of the Gender Policy of the GCF.
36. In the gender assessment, the AE provides information on the enabling environment to pursue gender equality and women's empowerment in the four selected countries. All four countries demonstrate, through their constitutions, their commitment to gender equality and equal opportunities for women in various aspects of each country's development agenda. The commitments are transposed into various country strategies, legislation and laws, with an overall commitment to mainstreaming gender across the various sectors in the countries, with some countries establishing a ministry of gender to be responsible for women's rights, children and family issues. Of the four countries targeted, two demonstrate better achievements in their gender equality status and two of the countries still have considerable work to do address the challenges of enabling women to attain equality in the various sectors. Some of the countries have to contend with the background of prevailing traditions, customs and religion which define more broadly the day-to-day life and behaviour of the people; while others have achieved levels of equality in many aspects but lag in areas of employment opportunity, participation in decision-making and representation in the political arena.
37. The gender assessment, which was collated through a literature review, provides evidence of the differences in the achievements on gender equality across the four countries; however, it also demonstrates that it is still a challenge for all the counties to achieve gender equality. Challenges and disparities still remain in women's ability to participate in decisionmaking, in labour markets, legal processes and stereotypes due to sociocultural norms. Further, women are often poorer than men and therefore are less able to adapt to risks in general and climate change risks in particular. Women in these countries are also highly dependent on natural resources to fulfil their gender-based roles of care of families (e.g. fetching and securing water, food, fuel for cooking). Changes and impacts on the surrounding environment significantly and directly affect these traditional roles and women's ability to fulfil them. While this is generally be true for all women, those in women-led households tend to be more affected by climate change and its impacts because these women tend to have less access to land, less access to diversified agricultural practices and less ability to engage in alternative employment opportunities. The assessment of the AE demonstrates the potential gender-related challenges of climate risk, in that increasing temperatures and reductions in rainfall will reduced the availability of water in water points, rivers and streams, and this will have direct impacts on women and girls in their roles of collecting and using water. Likewise, there are challenges because of the differences in access to and control over land and water resources in coastal zones, because women tend to gain access through inheritance rather than tenure and formal tenure systems, which has an impact on women's ability to make decisions on land use and development issues. This also affects and has a negative impact on women's ability to access credit and extension services. Lack of access to decision-making processes is not only limited to land-related issues: it extends to political decisions, and coastal zone and land-use management. In addition, there is further segregation of activities, particularly in the fishing industry, where men fish offshore while women fish close to shore and are involved in post-harvest activities, particularly in small-scale fisheries.
38. Women's roles both within the households and outside impose time constraints and limit their ability to access information, capacity-related support and services. The assessment also identified that there is a lack of sex-disaggregated data for one country and, despite evidence that shows interlinkages between gender-based violence and environmental factors, integration of this perspective remains limited in adaptation practice.
39. Further, the assessment notes the challenges that are faced by women as a result of their current position and roles, pointing out that there are various opportunities to influence the work of the project resulting from the way women interact and their knowledge of the resources. Women, as everyday users of natural resources, can easily identify changes to

<!-- image -->

- habitats, species abundance and distribution, and can single out factors relating to these changes. Women therefore have a significant role to play in habitat restoration which is an important EbA activity.
40. Given that the programme has not yet determined the priority of EbA themes and geographic sites where the programme will invest through CSOs, there is a need for the programme to undertake a further gender assessment. CSOs to be selected to engage in the project will be supported to address gender issues, because it is noted that the capacity of these various CSOs varies significantly and there is a need to provide sustained support to them.
41. The AE, as required by the Gender Policy of the GCF, has provided a gender action plan. The action plan contains activities, targets, indicators and timelines with corresponding budgets. The programme will include a gender expert who will be responsible for implementing the gender action plan, working with the gender focal points in the RITs and regional gender resource persons in the four countries. The gender action plan includes activities on identifying priority EbA activities that will (1) address gender-based challenges to climate change adaptation; (2) support the development of the gender action plan as part of the long-term vision; (3) provide training for CSOs; (4) develop training manuals on gender; and (5) conduct further applied research to improve understanding of gender issues in EbA, and develop knowledge products and best practice on gender mainstreaming in EbA.

## 4.3 Risks

## 4.3.1. Overall programme assessment

42. The funding proposal is for a USD 38 million grant for a programme that will be executed by CI. CI, as the Executing Entity, will implement the Programme through the Critical Ecosystem Partnership Fund (CEPF), a platform jointly established and managed by CI. The goal of the programme is to reduce the vulnerability of island populations in Comoros, Madagascar, Mauritius and Seychelles by securing the critical ecosystem services. The accredited entity, Agence Française de Développement (AFD), is co-founder of the programme. AFD and CI will arrange co-financing of USD 11 million in grants.
43. The majority of programme activities will be executed via subprojects funded by grants to CSOs. The success of the programme depends on the ability of CEPF to attract and select highquality CSOs that implement high-impact projects.

## 4.3.2. Accredited entity/executing entity capability to execute the current programme (medium risk)

44. The accredited entity, AFD, is a public international financial institution. AFD is rated as AA by Fitch and by Standard and Poor's, and has a strong track record of support by the French government. AFD has granted a total of USD 58 million to CEPF since its inception.
45. CI, acting through the Critical Ecosystem Partnership Fund - ('CEPF') shall be responsible for all the outputs and activities contained in the Funding Proposal. CEPF is a joint initiative of AFD, CI, the European Union, the Global Environment Facility, the Government of Japan and the World Bank. Implementation will be done via CSOs, whose capability will depend on CEPF's ability to select high quality entities. CEPF has a track record of sourcing and selecting over USD 232 million for biodiversity conservation to over 2,300 CSOs in 24 biodiversity hotspots. CEPF benefits from an established governance and operations.

## 4.3.3. Project-specific execution risks

<!-- image -->

GCF/B.26/02/Add.07

Page 81

46. Project sourcing and selection . The impact of the programme will be driven the ability by CEPF to generate a strong pipeline of projects to be implemented by CSOs. Projects are to be sourced via a call for proposals. CEPF has experience in the target area of the programme and local CSOs, and earlier calls for proposals indicate that there is a good appetite for participation. To access grants, projects must contribute to achieving the GCF investment criteria as per the initial eligibility criteria included in the Termsheet. CEPF will implement eligibility criteria consistent with the initial eligibility criteria. 47. Financial sustainability. The sustainability of the programme depends on the capacity of CI to generate additional revenues for EbA activities led by CSOs after the implementation period. Currently there is limited availability of such funding. Activities under Component 3 to support the long-term financial sustainability are intended to address this risk. 48. Political risk . Some countries in the hotspot have a recent history of political instability. Loss of political support for activities could reduce the ability of the programme to achieve its objectives. Comfort can be derived from the ability of CI to locate funds to other regions within the programme and to reduce dependency on local governments as a result of working with CSOs. 49. Co-financing risk. USD 8 million in co-financing still has to be fundraised. Inability to raise the funds would reduce the available funds and therefore would affect the impact of the programme. It is advised that all co-financing be committed before FAA effectiveness.

## 4.3.4. GCF portfolio concentration risk (low risk)

50. In case of approval, the impact of this proposal on the GCF portfolio concentration in terms of result area and single proposal is not material.

## 4.3.5. Compliance risk (medium risk)

51. The proposed activities under this project do not necessarily, in and of themselves, represent any unusually or unique high risk for money laundering or terrorist financing. Nevertheless, appropriate internal controls are necessary to ensure that any inherent risks for money laundering or terrorist financing, as well as for any prohibited practices, are properly identified, monitored, mitigated and corrected. The AE has indicated it has a strong internal control system in place to accomplish those obligations. Based on the information provided and in light of the proposed activities, the compliance risk is rated as medium.
